I don't feel energized at all. I might still be pacing around the kitchen because my legs won't stop bouncing and I can't just sit on the couch, but I am completely exhausted while I'm doing it. \n\nAnd as for doing or saying things faster than normal... I mean, my wife would probably tell you I *always* talk fast. Or at least, I talk fast when I've got a new idea in my head. Like with the hydroponic garden thing I mentioned earlier. I was trying to explain to her how the water pump connects to the PVC piping, and how you have to get the nutrient ratio just right or the roots will rot, and she had to physically put her hand on my arm and say, \"Breathe, you're talking a mile a minute.\" \n\nBut that's just how I am all the time. It's not like it comes on for three or four days and then goes away. It's just my normal speed. The guys at the station joke that I only have two gears: fast-forward and asleep, except I'm not very good at the asleep part. So, no, I don't have periods like what you're describing. It's just the same constant baseline every single day. Just always running.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or having trouble staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. All the time. It's actually getting to be a real problem, especially at the station. I'll be right in the middle of something important, like trying to fill out a run report—which, by the way, the new software the department just rolled out last month is a complete nightmare, it's got all these drop-down menus that don't make any sense and it takes twice as long—but I'll be sitting there, really trying to focus on it, right? I'm staring at the screen, telling myself, \"Just finish this report so you can go eat.\" \n\nBut then, out of nowhere, just the smallest thing will completely derail me. A radio chirp from dispatch—not even our tones dropping, just a random radio chirp for a different station—or somebody walking past the bay doors out of the corner of my eye, and my attention is just gone. I have to look. I have to see what it is. \n\nAnd honestly, half the time it doesn't even have to be something happening around me. It's my own brain. I'll just have a random thought pop into my head about a call from yesterday—like, \"Wait, did I remember to restock the pediatric non-rebreather masks in the airway bag after that asthma call?\"—and boom, I'm knocked completely off what I’m doing. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I lose that focus, it takes me forever to get back on track. I don't just go, \"Oh, right, the report,\" and go back to typing. No, I end up getting up, walking out to the engine to check the pediatric masks, and then while I'm out there in the bay, I notice a smudge on the diamond plate on the side of the rig, so I grab a rag to wipe it down. Then I see the rag bin is getting low, so I go to the laundry room... and an hour later, the lieutenant is asking me where my run report is, and I haven't typed a single word since I left the desk. It's like my brain is a TV and someone else is holding the remote, just mashing the channel button. I'm trying so hard to just look at one thing, but it just slips right away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt like you didn't need much sleep at all—like maybe only a few hours—and at the same time felt more energetic or like you could do a lot more than usual, even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, like I was saying a few minutes ago, it really doesn't work like that for me. I never get that magical second wind. Even if it's just for one night or a really short burst, if I don't sleep, I feel it. I feel it hard. \n\nTake just this past weekend, for example. My youngest, he's four, he had this awful ear infection. Just miserable, waking up crying every hour on the hour. My wife and I were taking turns walking him around the living room, so I maybe got three hours of sleep total, and it was broken up into these tiny, exhausting little twenty-minute chunks. And the next morning, I didn't feel energized at all. I felt like I had been run over by the ladder truck. My eyes were burning, my head was pounding, I felt physically sick from how tired I was. \n\nI mean, I was still doing stuff. I made pancakes for the kids, I went out back and tried to fix the broken latch on the fence, I probably started three different loads of laundry before noon. But I wasn't doing all of that because I felt energetic or because I thought I could take on the world. I was doing it because my brain just wouldn't let me sit down on the couch and actually rest. I was completely drained, but my body was just stuck on autopilot, buzzing around the house. \n\nSo I never have that feeling of not needing sleep and being super productive. Just always running.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or having trouble staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. All the time. It's actually getting to be a real problem, especially at the station. I'll be right in the middle of something important, like trying to fill out a run report—which, by the way, the new software the department just rolled out last month is a complete nightmare, it's got all these drop-down menus that don't make any sense and it takes twice as long—but I'll be sitting there, really trying to focus on it, right? I'm staring at the screen, telling myself, \"Just finish this report so you can go eat.\" \n\nBut then, out of nowhere, just the smallest thing will completely derail me. A radio chirp from dispatch—not even our tones dropping, just a random radio chirp for a different station—or somebody walking past the bay doors out of the corner of my eye, and my attention is just gone. I have to look. I have to see what it is. \n\nAnd honestly, half the time it doesn't even have to be something happening around me. It's my own brain. I'll just have a random thought pop into my head about a call from yesterday—like, \"Wait, did I remember to restock the pediatric non-rebreather masks in the airway bag after that asthma call?\"—and boom, I'm knocked completely off what I’m doing. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I lose that focus, it takes me forever to get back on track. I don't just go, \"Oh, right, the report,\" and go back to typing. No, I end up getting up, walking out to the engine to check the pediatric masks, and then while I'm out there in the bay, I notice a smudge on the diamond plate on the side of the rig, so I grab a rag to wipe it down. Then I see the rag bin is getting low, so I go to the laundry room... and an hour later, the lieutenant is asking me where my run report is, and I haven't typed a single word since I left the desk. It's like my brain is a TV and someone else is holding the remote, just mashing the channel button. I'm trying so hard to just look at one thing, but it just slips right away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt like you didn't need much sleep at all—like maybe only a few hours—and at the same time felt more energetic or like you could do a lot more than usual, even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, like I was saying a few minutes ago, it really doesn't work like that for me. I never get that magical second wind. Even if it's just for one night or a really short burst, if I don't sleep, I feel it. I feel it hard. \n\nTake just this past weekend, for example. My youngest, he's four, he had this awful ear infection. Just miserable, waking up crying every hour on the hour. My wife and I were taking turns walking him around the living room, so I maybe got three hours of sleep total, and it was broken up into these tiny, exhausting little twenty-minute chunks. And the next morning, I didn't feel energized at all. I felt like I had been run over by the ladder truck. My eyes were burning, my head was pounding, I felt physically sick from how tired I was. \n\nI mean, I was still doing stuff. I made pancakes for the kids, I went out back and tried to fix the broken latch on the fence, I probably started three different loads of laundry before noon. But I wasn't doing all of that because I felt energetic or because I thought I could take on the world. I was doing it because my brain just wouldn't let me sit down on the couch and actually rest. I was completely drained, but my body was just stuck on autopilot, buzzing around the house. \n\nSo I never have that feeling of not needing sleep and being super productive. It's just me desperately wanting to sleep, but my engine won't shut off, no matter how empty the gas tank is. So no, I've never experienced what you're talking about, not even for a short time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was unusually high or irritable for several days, and during that time you were much more active than usual, started a lot of projects, or felt driven to keep going even if you were tired?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, the irritable part... sure, I get irritable, especially when I'm running on empty after a brutal 48-hour shift and I come home and the kids are screaming at each other over a toy. But it's not some unusual, out-of-the-blue phase that lasts for days for no reason. It's just because I'm exhausted. \n\nAnd as for starting a lot of projects... Doc, you'd probably have to ask my wife about that, because she'll tell you my entire life is just one long, never-ending string of starting projects. It's not a special period of time where my mood is high, it's just how I operate every single day. \n\nJust look at our garage right now. I've got this half-built custom workbench completely taking up my wife's parking spot. I decided I needed to build it because the old one was too wobbly for when I'm trying to fix things around the house, so I went down to the hardware store, bought all this premium lumber, drew up these elaborate blueprints on the back of an old dispatch printout... and that was three months ago. I got the frame built, and then I realized I needed a specific type of countersink drill bit to make the top perfectly flush, so I stopped to go find one. But while I was digging through my tool chest looking for the bit, I found my old camping gear and suddenly realized I needed to waterproof the family tent before summer. So I dragged the tent out onto the driveway, and the workbench is just sitting there, unfinished, collecting dust. \n\nSo, yeah, I start a million projects. I'm always active, and I always feel driven to keep going even when I'm dead on my feet, like I was saying before. But it's not tied to my mood being unusually high, and it definitely doesn't just happen for a few days and then go away. It's just... me. It's constant. There's no on and off switch for it, no \"Oh, he's in a highly active mood this week.\" It's just the exact same exhausting pace, day in, day out. If anything, I wish I *could* have a few days where I didn't feel driven to start something new so I could actually finish the things I've already started. But no, it's not an episodic mood thing like you're describing at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't... I mean, I don't really think racing is the right word for it. When you say racing, it makes it sound like, I don't know, a runaway train? Like my thoughts are going a hundred miles an hour and I am sitting there panicking, trying to pull the emergency brake. It is not really like that at all. It is not this intense, overwhelming speed that suddenly hits me out of nowhere. 

It is more like... well, kind of like I was saying earlier with the TV remote. It is just constant switching. It is always happening. Like, just the other night, my wife and I finally had a night off together, the kids were actually asleep for once, and she wanted to watch this new detective show on TV. Supposed to be really good, won a bunch of awards or whatever. So we are sitting there on the couch, and my brain is not racing, it is just... wandering off, constantly. I am looking at the main character, and I notice his jacket, which makes me think about my own winter turnout gear, which makes me remember that the zipper on my station jacket is sticking. And then I am thinking, okay, I need to get some graphite spray or something for that zipper. Which reminds me that the hinges on the bay doors at the station were squeaking on Tuesday, and I meant to spray them, but then the tones dropped for a motor vehicle accident out on the highway, so I never did it. And then I start wondering if the other shift ever sprayed the hinges, because you know they never do the station maintenance unless you practically hold their hands...

So, you see what I mean? It is a lot of thoughts, sure, and they definitely bounce around from one completely unrelated thing to another. But they are not going so fast that I cannot keep up with them, and it is definitely not some special time where I suddenly cannot slow my brain down. It is just the normal, everyday noise. It is like that from the minute I wake up until the minute I finally pass out from exhaustion. There is no period of time where it suddenly shifts gears and speeds up into this uncontrollable racing feeling you are talking about. It is just a steady, never-ending stream of just... stuff. Always. So no, I really have not noticed times where they race like that.

It's really just this constant, unrelenting restlessness. Like I'm plugged into a wall socket, but the battery is still draining, if that makes sense. I don't just go, \"Oh, right, the report,\" and go back to typing. It's like my brain is a TV and someone else is holding the remote, just mashing the channel button. And the next morning, I didn't feel energized at all. I felt like I had been run over by the ladder truck. My eyes were burning, my head was pounding, I felt physically sick from how tired I was. \n\nI mean, I was still doing stuff. If anything, I wish I *could* have a few days where I didn't feel driven to start something new so I could actually finish the things I've already started. Supposed to be really good, won a bunch of awards or whatever. So we are sitting there on the couch, and my brain is not racing, it is just... wandering off, constantly. But it's never a situation where I think, \"Oh, I only got two hours, I feel amazing, let's go!\" \n\nNo, if I get two hours because we had a bad structure fire on the east side of town—which actually happened last month, this old abandoned warehouse went up and we were out there from midnight until shift change, just soaking it from the exterior because the roof was sagging—if that happens, I feel like absolute garbage the next day. I don't feel energized at all. I might still be pacing around the kitchen because my legs won't stop bouncing and I can't just sit on the couch, but I am completely exhausted while I'm doing it. \n\nAnd as for doing or saying things faster than normal... I mean, my wife would probably tell you I *always* talk fast. Or at least, I talk fast when I've got a new idea in my head. Like with the hydroponic garden thing I mentioned earlier. I was trying to explain to her how the water pump connects to the PVC piping, and how you have to get the nutrient ratio just right or the roots will rot, and she had to physically put her hand on my arm and say, \"Breathe, you're talking a mile a minute.\" \n\nBut that's just how I am all the time. It's not like it comes on for three or four days and then goes away. It's just my normal speed. The guys at the station joke that I only have two gears: fast-forward and asleep, except I'm not very good at the asleep part. So, no, I don't have periods like what you're describing. It's just the same constant baseline every single day. Just always running.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or having trouble staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. All the time. It's actually getting to be a real problem, especially at the station. I'll be right in the middle of something important, like trying to fill out a run report—which, by the way, the new software the department just rolled out last month is a complete nightmare, it's got all these drop-down menus that don't make any sense and it takes twice as long—but I'll be sitting there, really trying to focus on it, right? I'm staring at the screen, telling myself, \"Just finish this report so you can go eat.\" \n\nBut then, out of nowhere, just the smallest thing will completely derail me. A radio chirp from dispatch—not even our tones dropping, just a random radio chirp for a different station—or somebody walking past the bay doors out of the corner of my eye, and my attention is just gone. I have to look. I have to see what it is. \n\nAnd honestly, half the time it doesn't even have to be something happening around me. It's my own brain. I'll just have a random thought pop into my head about a call from yesterday—like, \"Wait, did I remember to restock the pediatric non-rebreather masks in the airway bag after that asthma call?\"—and boom, I'm knocked completely off what I’m doing. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I lose that focus, it takes me forever to get back on track. I don't just go, \"Oh, right, the report,\" and go back to typing. No, I end up getting up, walking out to the engine to check the pediatric masks, and then while I'm out there in the bay, I notice a smudge on the diamond plate on the side of the rig, so I grab a rag to wipe it down. Then I see the rag bin is getting low, so I go to the laundry room... and an hour later, the lieutenant is asking me where my run report is, and I haven't typed a single word since I left the desk. It's like my brain is a TV and someone else is holding the remote, just mashing the channel button. I'm trying so hard to just look at one thing, but it just slips right away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt like you didn't need much sleep at all—like maybe only a few hours—and at the same time felt more energetic or like you could do a lot more than usual, even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, like I was saying a few minutes ago, it really doesn't work like that for me. I never get that magical second wind. Even if it's just for one night or a really short burst, if I don't sleep, I feel it. I feel it hard. \n\nTake just this past weekend, for example. My youngest, he's four, he had this awful ear infection. Just miserable, waking up crying every hour on the hour. My wife and I were taking turns walking him around the living room, so I maybe got three hours of sleep total, and it was broken up into these tiny, exhausting little twenty-minute chunks. And the next morning, I didn't feel energized at all. I felt like I had been run over by the ladder truck. My eyes were burning, my head was pounding, I felt physically sick from how tired I was. \n\nI mean, I was still doing stuff. I made pancakes for the kids, I went out back and tried to fix the broken latch on the fence, I probably started three different loads of laundry before noon. But I wasn't doing all of that because I felt energetic or because I thought I could take on the world. I was doing it because my brain just wouldn't let me sit down on the couch and actually rest. I was completely drained, but my body was just stuck on autopilot, buzzing around the house. \n\nSo I never have that feeling of not needing sleep and being super productive. It's just me desperately wanting to sleep, but my engine won't shut off, no matter how empty the gas tank is. So no, I've never experienced what you're talking about, not even for a short time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was unusually high or irritable for several days, and during that time you were much more active than usual, started a lot of projects, or felt driven to keep going even if you were tired?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, the irritable part... sure, I get irritable, especially when I'm running on empty after a brutal 48-hour shift and I come home and the kids are screaming at each other over a toy. But it's not some unusual, out-of-the-blue phase that lasts for days for no reason. It's just because I'm exhausted. \n\nAnd as for starting a lot of projects... Doc, you'd probably have to ask my wife about that, because she'll tell you my entire life is just one long, never-ending string of starting projects. It's not a special period of time where my mood is high, it's just how I operate every single day. \n\nJust look at our garage right now. I've got this half-built custom workbench completely taking up my wife's parking spot. I decided I needed to build it because the old one was too wobbly for when I'm trying to fix things around the house, so I went down to the hardware store, bought all this premium lumber, drew up these elaborate blueprints on the back of an old dispatch printout... and that was three months ago. I got the frame built, and then I realized I needed a specific type of countersink drill bit to make the top perfectly flush, so I stopped to go find one. But while I was digging through my tool chest looking for the bit, I found my old camping gear and suddenly realized I needed to waterproof the family tent before summer. So I dragged the tent out onto the driveway, and the workbench is just sitting there, unfinished, collecting dust. \n\nSo, yeah, I start a million projects. I'm always active, and I always feel driven to keep going even when I'm dead on my feet, like I was saying before. But it's not tied to my mood being unusually high, and it definitely doesn't just happen for a few days and then go away. It's just... me. It's constant. There's no on and off switch for it, no \"Oh, he's in a highly active mood this week.\" It's just the exact same exhausting pace, day in, day out. If anything, I wish I *could* have a few days where I didn't feel driven to start something new so I could actually finish the things I've already started. But no, it's not an episodic mood thing like you're describing at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when your thoughts were racing so much that you couldn’t slow them down, even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I don't... I mean, I don't really think racing is the right word for it. When you say racing, it makes it sound like, I don't know, a runaway train? Like my thoughts are going a hundred miles an hour and I am sitting there panicking, trying to pull the emergency brake. It is not really like that at all. It is not this intense, overwhelming speed that suddenly hits me out of nowhere. \n\nIt is more like... well, kind of like I was saying earlier with the TV remote. It is just constant switching. It is always happening. Like, just the other night, my wife and I finally had a night off together, the kids were actually asleep for once, and she wanted to watch this new detective show on TV. Supposed to be really good, won a bunch of awards or whatever. So we are sitting there on the couch, and my brain is not racing, it is just... wandering off, constantly. I am looking at the main character, and I notice his jacket, which makes me think about my own winter turnout gear, which makes me remember that the zipper on my station jacket is sticking. And then I am thinking, okay, I need to get some graphite spray or something for that zipper. Which reminds me that the hinges on the bay doors at the station were squeaking on Tuesday, and I meant to spray them, but then the tones dropped for a motor vehicle accident out on the highway, so I never did it. And then I start wondering if the other shift ever sprayed the hinges, because you know they never do the station maintenance unless you practically hold their hands...\n\nSo, you see what I mean? It is a lot of thoughts, sure, and they definitely bounce around from one completely unrelated thing to another. But they are not going so fast that I cannot keep up with them, and it is definitely not some special time where I suddenly cannot slow my brain down. It is just the normal, everyday noise. It is like that from the minute I wake up until the minute I finally pass out from exhaustion. There is no period of time where it suddenly shifts gears and speeds up into this uncontrollable racing feeling you are talking about. It is just a steady, never-ending stream of just... stuff. Always. So no, I really have not noticed times where they race like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly feel like we're kind of covering the same ground here, because it's just like I was saying a minute ago about the projects and the sleep. There is no special 'time' or phase where this happens. It's not like I wake up one Tuesday and suddenly I'm this highly elevated, super talkative, impulsive guy for a week, and then I go back to normal. That is just my normal. That's my every day. 

Am I talkative? Oh, absolutely. Ask any of the guys at the firehouse. We'll be sitting around the kitchen table after shift change, just having coffee, and I will talk their ears off about literally anything. Last week I went down this massive internet rabbit hole about the history of suspension bridges—don't ask me why, I don't even know how I got there, I think I was looking up something about ropes for a rigging drill—but I spent forty-five minutes explaining the engineering of the Brooklyn Bridge to my crew until Captain Miller finally told me to go check the air packs on the engine just to get me to stop talking. 

And impulsive? Yeah, my wife will be the first to tell you that. I'll suddenly decide we need to rearrange the entire living room at 10 PM on a Sunday, or I'll buy things we don't need. A couple of weeks ago, I bought a used commercial-grade power washer off Facebook Marketplace at two in the morning because I watched a video of someone cleaning a patio and decided I had to do our driveway the very next day. I literally drove forty miles the next morning to pick it up before my shift started. 

But the thing is, Doc, none of that is tied to some elevated mood, and it doesn't just last for a few days. It's constant. It's just who I am, all the time, day in and day out. And like I said before, I never feel like I need less sleep during any of this. I'm usually dead tired while I'm doing it, running on fumes. So no, I've never had a specific period of time like you're describing. It's just my baseline.

I still love my job, even with the captain breathing down my neck. And I still love spending time with my wife and the kids. Actually, if anything, I probably want to do *too much* with them. \n\nLike, last weekend, I had this whole plan. I was going to take the boys fishing at the lake early in the morning—because they've been asking to go since, gosh, probably April—and then I figured we could come back, fire up the grill, have some burgers, and then maybe hit the batting cages in the afternoon. And my wife was just like, \"Can we just have one quiet Saturday where we hang out in the backyard and don't have an itinerary?\" And I just... I can't do that. Sitting in the backyard just staring at the grass makes me feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin. I have to be doing something. I have to be engaged. \n\nSo, I still have plenty of interest in my hobbies. I've got a whole garage full of half-finished projects to prove it. I started rebuilding this old motorcycle—a '78 Honda CB750, beautiful bike—but then I got really into woodworking for about a month and built half a bookshelf, and now I'm trying to figure out how to set up a hydroponic garden in the basement. I love all of it, I really do. I just can't seem to stick with one thing long enough to finish it before I'm on to the next. But no, no hopelessness or losing interest. I'm just... I'm just always on the go, trying to find the next thing to do. So, yeah, mood-wise, I think I'm doing okay, all things considered. It's just the physical... the motor, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or so confident that you didn’t sleep for days but still didn’t feel tired?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I don't ever feel like I'm on top of the world or invincible or anything. And like I said before, I definitely feel tired. I'm exhausted a lot of the time, especially with the crazy shift schedule we have at the firehouse. Sometimes we'll get a run of back-to-back calls right around 2:00 AM, and by the time 7:00 AM rolls around, my eyes are practically bleeding, I'm so tired. \n\nSo it's never a case of just not *needing* sleep or going days without it and feeling totally fine. I know some guys who can pull off those 48-hour shifts and still go play softball on their day off, but I'm not like that. I want to sleep. I crave it. It's just that my body won't cooperate when I actually try to get it. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually high or overly confident... nah. I mean, I'm confident in my skills as a firefighter. You kind of have to be when you're walking into a burning building, right? You have to trust your training and your crew. But it's not like an abnormal, super-inflated ego thing where I think I'm Superman. I know my limits. In fact, lately, with all the mistakes I've been making—like misplacing the thermal imaging camera last week, which was a whole ordeal because the lieutenant was furious, and I had to spend an hour tearing apart the engine to find it—my confidence is actually taking a bit of a hit. \n\nSo yeah, no periods of feeling high or not needing sleep for days. It's really just this constant, unrelenting restlessness. Like I'm plugged into a wall socket, but the battery is still draining, if that makes sense. I'm just running on fumes half the time, but the engine won't shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt so unusually irritable or excited that it was hard to control your emotions or behavior, even if it didn't last very long?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I definitely get irritable, sure. Who wouldn't, right? Especially when I've been up all night on shift, or when I'm trying to get one of my kids to put their shoes on for the fourth time and they're just staring at me like I'm speaking a different language. But it's not like some crazy, out-of-control thing where I'm flying off the handle or feeling so excited that I can't contain myself. It's more just... regular frustration, I guess. \n\nLike, the other day at the station, we were doing morning rig checks, and someone—I won't name names, but he's a rookie—left the medical bag completely unstocked from the shift before. Just empty wrappers and used flushes shoved back in there. And yeah, I got annoyed. I snapped at him a little bit, told him he needed to pay better attention because that kind of thing can actually cost someone their life out on a call. But I wasn't, like, raging or anything. I didn't lose control. I just told him to fix it, and then I went and made a pot of coffee because I was irritated and needed to do something with my hands. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually excited... no. I mean, I get excited about things, like when I finally got that Honda CB750 engine to turn over after working on it for three weeks, but it's just normal excitement. It's not like this overwhelming wave where I can't control my behavior. I'm always moving, always doing something, but I'm still in the driver's seat, you know? It's just that the car won't stop moving. But emotionally? No, I don't think I've ever had a period where I felt so irritable or excited that I lost control. Just the normal day-to-day stuff, maybe amplified a little by how tired I always am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energized, needed much less sleep than usual, and were doing or saying things faster than normal, even if you weren’t actually sleeping more or feeling better rested?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, not like a specific block of days where suddenly I'm a different person with all this extra energy. Like I was saying about the sleep thing, I definitely need my sleep. I'm always tired. It's just that I can't always get my brain to shut off to actually get the rest. But it's never a situation where I think, \"Oh, I only got two hours, I feel amazing, let's go!\" \n\nNo, if I get two hours because we had a bad structure fire on the east side of town—which actually happened last month, this old abandoned warehouse went up and we were out there from midnight until shift change, just soaking it from the exterior because the roof was sagging—if that happens, I feel like absolute garbage the next day. I don't feel energized at all. I might still be pacing around the kitchen because my legs won't stop bouncing and I can't just sit on the couch, but I am completely exhausted while I'm doing it. \n\nAnd as for doing or saying things faster than normal... I mean, my wife would probably tell you I *always* talk fast. Or at least, I talk fast when I've got a new idea in my head. Like with the hydroponic garden thing I mentioned earlier. I was trying to explain to her how the water pump connects to the PVC piping, and how you have to get the nutrient ratio just right or the roots will rot, and she had to physically put her hand on my arm and say, \"Breathe, you're talking a mile a minute.\" \n\nBut that's just how I am all the time. It's not like it comes on for three or four days and then goes away. It's just my normal speed. The guys at the station joke that I only have two gears: fast-forward and asleep, except I'm not very good at the asleep part. So, no, I don't have periods like what you're describing. It's just the same constant baseline every single day. Just always running.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or having trouble staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. All the time. It's actually getting to be a real problem, especially at the station. I'll be right in the middle of something important, like trying to fill out a run report—which, by the way, the new software the department just rolled out last month is a complete nightmare, it's got all these drop-down menus that don't make any sense and it takes twice as long—but I'll be sitting there, really trying to focus on it, right? I'm staring at the screen, telling myself, \"Just finish this report so you can go eat.\" \n\nBut then, out of nowhere, just the smallest thing will completely derail me. A radio chirp from dispatch—not even our tones dropping, just a random radio chirp for a different station—or somebody walking past the bay doors out of the corner of my eye, and my attention is just gone. I have to look. I have to see what it is. \n\nAnd honestly, half the time it doesn't even have to be something happening around me. It's my own brain. I'll just have a random thought pop into my head about a call from yesterday—like, \"Wait, did I remember to restock the pediatric non-rebreather masks in the airway bag after that asthma call?\"—and boom, I'm knocked completely off what I’m doing. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I lose that focus, it takes me forever to get back on track. I don't just go, \"Oh, right, the report,\" and go back to typing. No, I end up getting up, walking out to the engine to check the pediatric masks, and then while I'm out there in the bay, I notice a smudge on the diamond plate on the side of the rig, so I grab a rag to wipe it down. Then I see the rag bin is getting low, so I go to the laundry room... and an hour later, the lieutenant is asking me where my run report is, and I haven't typed a single word since I left the desk. It's like my brain is a TV and someone else is holding the remote, just mashing the channel button. I'm trying so hard to just look at one thing, but it just slips right away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt like you didn't need much sleep at all—like maybe only a few hours—and at the same time felt more energetic or like you could do a lot more than usual, even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, like I was saying a few minutes ago, it really doesn't work like that for me. I never get that magical second wind. Even if it's just for one night or a really short burst, if I don't sleep, I feel it. I feel it hard. \n\nTake just this past weekend, for example. My youngest, he's four, he had this awful ear infection. Just miserable, waking up crying every hour on the hour. My wife and I were taking turns walking him around the living room, so I maybe got three hours of sleep total, and it was broken up into these tiny, exhausting little twenty-minute chunks. And the next morning, I didn't feel energized at all. I felt like I had been run over by the ladder truck. My eyes were burning, my head was pounding, I felt physically sick from how tired I was. \n\nI mean, I was still doing stuff. I made pancakes for the kids, I went out back and tried to fix the broken latch on the fence, I probably started three different loads of laundry before noon. But I wasn't doing all of that because I felt energetic or because I thought I could take on the world. I was doing it because my brain just wouldn't let me sit down on the couch and actually rest. I was completely drained, but my body was just stuck on autopilot, buzzing around the house. \n\nSo I never have that feeling of not needing sleep and being super productive. It's just me desperately wanting to sleep, but my engine won't shut off, no matter how empty the gas tank is. So no, I've never experienced what you're talking about, not even for a short time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was unusually high or irritable for several days, and during that time you were much more active than usual, started a lot of projects, or felt driven to keep going even if you were tired?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, the irritable part... sure, I get irritable, especially when I'm running on empty after a brutal 48-hour shift and I come home and the kids are screaming at each other over a toy. But it's not some unusual, out-of-the-blue phase that lasts for days for no reason. It's just because I'm exhausted. \n\nAnd as for starting a lot of projects... Doc, you'd probably have to ask my wife about that, because she'll tell you my entire life is just one long, never-ending string of starting projects. It's not a special period of time where my mood is high, it's just how I operate every single day. \n\nJust look at our garage right now. I've got this half-built custom workbench completely taking up my wife's parking spot. I got the frame built, and then I realized I needed a specific type of countersink drill bit to make the top perfectly flush, so I stopped to go find one. But while I was digging through my tool chest looking for the bit, I found my old camping gear and suddenly realized I needed to waterproof the family tent before summer. So I dragged the tent out onto the driveway, and the workbench is just sitting there, unfinished, collecting dust. \n\nSo, yeah, I start a million projects. I'm always active, and I always feel driven to keep going even when I'm dead on my feet, like I was saying before. There's no on and off switch for it, no \"Oh, he's in a highly active mood this week.\" It's just the exact same exhausting pace, day in, day out. If anything, I wish I *could* have a few days where I didn't feel driven to start something new so I could actually finish the things I've already started. But no, it's not an episodic mood thing like you're describing at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when your thoughts were racing so much that you couldn’t slow them down, even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I don't... I mean, I don't really think racing is the right word for it. When you say racing, it makes it sound like, I don't know, a runaway train? It is not this intense, overwhelming speed that suddenly hits me out of nowhere. \n\nIt is more like... well, kind of like I was saying earlier with the TV remote. It is just constant switching. It is always happening. Like, just the other night, my wife and I finally had a night off together, the kids were actually asleep for once, and she wanted to watch this new detective show on TV. Supposed to be really good, won a bunch of awards or whatever. So we are sitting there on the couch, and my brain is not racing, it is just... wandering off, constantly. And then I am thinking, okay, I need to get some graphite spray or something for that zipper. Which reminds me that the hinges on the bay doors at the station were squeaking on Tuesday, and I meant to spray them, but then the tones dropped for a motor vehicle accident out on the highway, so I never did it. It's not like I wake up one Tuesday and suddenly I'm this highly elevated, super talkative, impulsive guy for a week, and then I go back to normal. That is just my normal. That's my every day. \n\nAm I talkative? Oh, absolutely. Ask any of the guys at the firehouse. We'll be sitting around the kitchen table after shift change, just having coffee, and I will talk their ears off about literally anything. Last week I went down this massive internet rabbit hole about the history of suspension bridges—don't ask me why, I don't even know how I got there, I think I was looking up something about ropes for a rigging drill—but I spent forty-five minutes explaining the engineering of the Brooklyn Bridge to my crew until Captain Miller finally told me to go check the air packs on the engine just to get me to stop talking. \n\nAnd impulsive? Yeah, my wife will be the first to tell you that. I'll suddenly decide we need to rearrange the entire living room at 10 PM on a Sunday, or I'll buy things we don't need. A couple of weeks ago, I bought a used commercial-grade power washer off Facebook Marketplace at two in the morning because I watched a video of someone cleaning a patio and decided I had to do our driveway the very next day. I literally drove forty miles the next morning to pick it up before my shift started. \n\nBut the thing is, Doc, none of that is tied to some elevated mood, and it doesn't just last for a few days. It's constant. It's just who I am, all the time, day in and day out. And like I said before, I never feel like I need less sleep during any of this. I'm usually dead tired while I'm doing it, running on fumes. So no, I've never had a specific period of time like you're describing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worrying? No, not really. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by worry, but if you're talking about sitting around stressing over little things that aren't a big deal... no, that's just not me. Honestly, Doc, I don't think my brain stays on one track long enough to actually build up a good worry about anything. 

In my line of work, you know, you can't really afford to be a worrywart anyway. When the tones drop and we're heading out to a fully involved structure fire or a bad pileup on the interstate, yeah, your adrenaline is pumping, and you're hyper-focused on the job, but it's not that spinning-your-wheels kind of anxiety. You just do what you're trained to do. 

And as for everyday stuff at home... I just don't get stuck on things. My wife, she's the worrier in the family. She'll lay awake at night stressing about whether we're putting enough away for the kids' college, or if the roof is going to hold up through the winter, or if she said the wrong thing to her sister at Thanksgiving. But me? I'm just moving too fast to sit there and stew on it. 

Take my truck, for example. About a month ago, the check engine light came on. Now, a lot of guys would stress about that, right? They'd be up at night wondering if the transmission is shot, how much the mechanic is going to gouge them for, if they need to figure out a rental car to get to work. Me? I looked at the dashboard, thought, "Well, that's a problem for future me," and then I got distracted by a song on the radio and completely forgot the light was even on. Literally forgot about it until the truck actually stalled out in the grocery store parking lot three weeks later. I was annoyed, sure, especially because I had a whole cart of groceries and the ice cream was melting and I had to call my buddy from the station to come give me a tow with his rig... it was a whole ordeal. But the point is, I wasn't sitting up at night *worrying* about the light before it happened. 

So, no, I don't get trapped in my head worrying about things I can't turn off. Like I was saying before, my thoughts are always jumping from one thing to the next, so I'm usually onto a completely different thought or project before I even have the time to get anxious about the first thing. I don't really have that "stuck" feeling with worrying at all.

Supposed to be really good, won a bunch of awards or whatever. So we are sitting there on the couch, and my brain is not racing, it is just... wandering off, constantly. I am looking at the main character, and I notice his jacket, which makes me think about my own winter turnout gear, which makes me remember that the zipper on my station jacket is sticking. And then I am thinking, okay, I need to get some graphite spray or something for that zipper. Which reminds me that the hinges on the bay doors at the station were squeaking on Tuesday, and I meant to spray them, but then the tones dropped for a motor vehicle accident out on the highway, so I never did it. And then I start wondering if the other shift ever sprayed the hinges, because you know they never do the station maintenance unless you practically hold their hands...\n\nSo, you see what I mean? It is a lot of thoughts, sure, and they definitely bounce around from one completely unrelated thing to another. Always. So no, I really have not noticed times where they race like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was elevated or irritable for at least a few days, and during that time you were more talkative than usual, needed less sleep, or were doing things more quickly or impulsively than normal?\n\nPatient: I honestly feel like we're kind of covering the same ground here, because it's just like I was saying a minute ago about the projects and the sleep. It's not like I wake up one Tuesday and suddenly I'm this highly elevated, super talkative, impulsive guy for a week, and then I go back to normal. That is just my normal. I literally drove forty miles the next morning to pick it up before my shift started. \n\nBut the thing is, Doc, none of that is tied to some elevated mood, and it doesn't just last for a few days. It's constant. It's just who I am, all the time, day in and day out. And like I said before, I never feel like I need less sleep during any of this. I'm usually dead tired while I'm doing it, running on fumes. So no, I've never had a specific period of time like you're describing. Honestly, Doc, I don't think my brain stays on one track long enough to actually build up a good worry about anything. \n\nIn my line of work, you know, you can't really afford to be a worrywart anyway. When the tones drop and we're heading out to a fully involved structure fire or a bad pileup on the interstate, yeah, your adrenaline is pumping, and you're hyper-focused on the job, but it's not that spinning-your-wheels kind of anxiety. You just do what you're trained to do. \n\nAnd as for everyday stuff at home... I just don't get stuck on things. My wife, she's the worrier in the family. She'll lay awake at night stressing about whether we're putting enough away for the kids' college, or if the roof is going to hold up through the winter, or if she said the wrong thing to her sister at Thanksgiving. But me? I'm just moving too fast to sit there and stew on it. \n\nTake my truck, for example. About a month ago, the check engine light came on. Now, a lot of guys would stress about that, right? They'd be up at night wondering if the transmission is shot, how much the mechanic is going to gouge them for, if they need to figure out a rental car to get to work. Me? I looked at the dashboard, thought, \"Well, that's a problem for future me,\" and then I got distracted by a song on the radio and completely forgot the light was even on. Literally forgot about it until the truck actually stalled out in the grocery store parking lot three weeks later. I was annoyed, sure, especially because I had a whole cart of groceries and the ice cream was melting and I had to call my buddy from the station to come give me a tow with his rig... it was a whole ordeal. Like I was saying before, my thoughts are always jumping from one thing to the next, so I'm usually onto a completely different thought or project before I even have the time to get anxious about the first thing. I don't really have that \"stuck\" feeling with worrying at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I'm usually dead tired while I'm doing it, running on fumes. So no, I've never had a specific period of time like you're describing. It's just my baseline.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know they may not be that big of a deal, and does it feel like you can’t turn off the worry even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Worrying? No, not really. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by worry, but if you're talking about sitting around stressing over little things that aren't a big deal... no, that's just not me. Honestly, Doc, I don't think my brain stays on one track long enough to actually build up a good worry about anything. \n\nIn my line of work, you know, you can't really afford to be a worrywart anyway. When the tones drop and we're heading out to a fully involved structure fire or a bad pileup on the interstate, yeah, your adrenaline is pumping, and you're hyper-focused on the job, but it's not that spinning-your-wheels kind of anxiety. You just do what you're trained to do. \n\nAnd as for everyday stuff at home... I just don't get stuck on things. My wife, she's the worrier in the family. She'll lay awake at night stressing about whether we're putting enough away for the kids' college, or if the roof is going to hold up through the winter, or if she said the wrong thing to her sister at Thanksgiving. But me? I'm just moving too fast to sit there and stew on it. \n\nTake my truck, for example. About a month ago, the check engine light came on. Now, a lot of guys would stress about that, right? They'd be up at night wondering if the transmission is shot, how much the mechanic is going to gouge them for, if they need to figure out a rental car to get to work. Me? I looked at the dashboard, thought, \"Well, that's a problem for future me,\" and then I got distracted by a song on the radio and completely forgot the light was even on. Literally forgot about it until the truck actually stalled out in the grocery store parking lot three weeks later. I was annoyed, sure, especially because I had a whole cart of groceries and the ice cream was melting and I had to call my buddy from the station to come give me a tow with his rig... it was a whole ordeal. But the point is, I wasn't sitting up at night *worrying* about the light before it happened. \n\nSo, no, I don't get trapped in my head worrying about things I can't turn off. Like I was saying before, my thoughts are always jumping from one thing to the next, so I'm usually onto a completely different thought or project before I even have the time to get anxious about the first thing. I don't really have that \"stuck\" feeling with worrying at all."
